### Vatia CFMP Reserve

--- SENTENCE 2759 ---
### Overview

The village of Vatia is located on the north-central coast of Tutuila and partially overlaps the Tutuila unit of the National Park.

--- SENTENCE 2760 ---
The Vatia CFMP reserve (Figure 5.46) was established in 2001 to "manage, protect, and preserve the fish, shellfish, and the coastal area of the village of Vatia" (ASDMWR 2002b).

--- SENTENCE 2761 ---
The ~0.6 km² reserve extends from Falelofia Point at the northern end of Polatai Islet to Craggy Point at the boundary with Afono village and offshore approximately 100 yards (ASDMWR 2002b).

--- SENTENCE 2762 ---
It primarily fronts a ~4.9 km² watershed in minimally impacted condition with the population concentrated around Vatia Bay.

--- SENTENCE 2763 ---
Because of the soil types and steep slopes, there is moderate to high potential for periodic natural erosion and nearshore sediment impacts.

### **Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary**

### Overview

Located on the SW side of Tutuila, Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary (FBNMS)

--- SENTENCE 2791 ---
(Figure 5.49) encompasses ~0.7 km² of fringing coral reef ecosystem within a collapsed volcanic crater and extends from the southern tip of Fagatele Point to the southern tip of Step's Point.

--- SENTENCE 2792 ---
The Sanctuary was designated in 1986 to "protect and preserve an example of a pristine tropical marine habitat and coral reef terrace ecosystem" (15 C.F.R. 922.100-104, FBNMS Regulations 1986).

--- SENTENCE 2793 ---
FBNMS fronts a ~3.2 km² watershed in pristine condition.

--- SENTENCE 2794 ---
Human impacts within the watershed are minimal and although nearshore waters may be affected by runoff of highly erosive soils on the steep slopes of the watershed, these runoffs are not associated with significant nutrient loads.

--- SENTENCE 2795 ---
There is a low density of pigs in the

--- SENTENCE 2802 ---
Fishing is presently regulated differently within two zones in FBNMS but may soon be modified to complete no-take with a management plan revision that is currently underway.

--- SENTENCE 2804 ---

### National Park of American Samoa - Ofu Unit

### Overview

The Ofu unit of the National Park (Figure 5.52) was authorized by Public Law 100-571 in 1988 and formally established in 1993 following a lease agreement with the villages (16 U.S.C. 410qq-410qq-1, NPS 1997).

--- SENTENCE 2827 ---
Its boundary follows the southeast shoreline road of Ofu Island from Fatauana Point to Asega Strait and extends 0.25 miles offshore.

--- SENTENCE 2828 ---
It also extends inland to include the southern slopes of Sunu'itao Peak (16 U.S.C. 410qq-410qq-1, NPS 1997).

--- SENTENCE 2829 ---
The 1986-7 NPS feasibility study noted the "exceptionally diverse reef fish and coral communities" in this area as well as the lack of reef damage from crown-of-thorns starfish outbreaks (NPS 1988).

--- SENTENCE 2830 ---
The ~1.5 km² marine portion of the Ofu park unit fronts the largely uninhabited southeast side of a ~4.4 km² watershed.

--- SENTENCE 2831 ---
The nearshore waters are not significantly impacted by human activity but may be impacted by natural sediment runoffs because of the steep slopes and highly erosive soils.

### National Park of American Samoa - Ta'u Unit

### Overview

The Ta'u unit of the National Park (Figure 5.55) was authorized by Public Law 100-571 in 1988 and formally established in 1993 following a lease agreement with the villages (16 U.S.C. 410qq-410qq-1, NPS 1997).

--- SENTENCE 2856 ---
It occupies ~20 km² of land and ~ 4.7 km² of marine habitats in the south and southeast of Ta'u Island.

--- SENTENCE 2857 ---
The 1986-7 NPS feasibility study noted that this area includes "the largest extent of both undisturbed lowland and montane rainforest and cloud forest left in American Samoa" and would "provide important habitat for seabirds, shorebirds, flying foxes, and forest birds" (NPS 1988).

--- SENTENCE 2858 ---
Also noted is the presence of the prehistoric village of Saua, along the east coast, and Taisamasama, or the Yellow Waters of Tui Manu'a cultural site, located centrally on the south shore.

--- SENTENCE 2859 ---
The marine component of the park has a seaward boundary that extends 0.25 miles offshore from Si'ufa'alele Point eastward to the Saua site (16 U.S.C. 410qq-410qq-1, NPS 1997).

--- SENTENCE 2860 ---
Most of the marine component of the park fronts an uninhabited ~8.5

--- SENTENCE 2867 ---
Although there are no real human impacts to the nearshore waters, there is a moderate to severe potential for sediment runoffs because of the steep slopes and highly erosive soils.

--- SENTENCE 2868 ---
There is a low density of pigs in the watershed and the portion actually fronting the park unit is uninhabited, so waste discharge from piggeries is less likely to impact nearshore waters.

--- SENTENCE 2869 ---

### Rose Atoll Marine National Monument and National Wildlife Refuge

--- SENTENCE 2920 ---
### Overview

Rose Atoll Marine National Monument (MNM) lies at the eastern end of the Samoan archipelago and was established in 2009 by Presidential Proclamation 8337 to protect the "lands, submerged lands, waters, and marine environment around Rose Atoll" and its "dynamic reef ecosystem that is home to a very diverse assemblage of terrestrial and marine species, many of which are threatened or endangered" (Proclamation No. 8337).

--- SENTENCE 2921 ---
The Monument has a rectangular seaward boundary approximately 50 nautical miles from the mean low water line of Rose Atoll (Figure 5.61).

--- SENTENCE 2922 ---
The volcanic hotspot responsible for the Samoan Island chain, the Vailulu'u seamount, lies just west of the monument (Appendix A).

--- SENTENCE 2923 ---
The Monument encompasses almost 35,000 km², including Rose Atoll National Wildlife Refuge (NWR), which was established by cooperative agreement between the Government of American Samoa and the USFWS in 1973 and includes the ~6.8 km²

!

--- SENTENCE 2924 ---
Mapped benthic habitat (by structure type) and fish and coral survey data within Rose Atoll MNM.

--- SENTENCE 2925 ---
Coral cover, coral richness, fish biomass, and fish richness values at each survey site are classified as high (red shading), medium (pink shading), or low (white shading).

--- SENTENCE 2926 ---
Grey shading indicates variables with no data at a given site.

--- SENTENCE 2927 ---
Fish and coral survey data are from CRSR and REA.

of land, submerged land, and waters within the mean low water line of Rose Atoll (RANWR 1974).

--- SENTENCE 2928 ---
Rose is one of the smallest atolls in the world and is uninhabited by humans.

--- SENTENCE 2929 ---
It provides important nesting grounds for the threatened green sea turtle and habitat for 17 species of federally protected migratory seabirds and shorebirds.

--- SENTENCE 2930 ---
It also supports the largest population of giant clams in American Samoa as well as many rare species of reef fish (Gombos et al. 2007).

--- SENTENCE 2931 ---
Because Rose Atoll is uninhabited, nearshore waters are not impacted by human use such as from urban runoffs and waste discharge from piggeries.

--- SENTENCE 2932 ---
Rose Atoll NWR has been closed to the public since its establishment to protect the fish and wildlife in the refuge and is managed exclusively by USFWS.

--- SENTENCE 2933 ---
Proclamation 8337 prohibits commercial fishing within the Monument and gives the Secretary of Commerce (through NOAA) primary management authority over fishery-related activities in the marine areas outside of the NWR (16 U.S.C. 1801 et seq., Proclamation No. 8337).

--- SENTENCE 2934 ---
The Secretary of Commerce has initiated the process to add the marine areas of the Monument to Fagatele Bay NMS in accordance with the National Marine Sanctuaries Act (16 U.S.C. 1431 et seq.).

--- SENTENCE 2935 ---
# Habitat Composition, Reef Fish, and Coral Communities

Most of the ~35000 km<sup>2</sup> within Rose Atoll MNM is open ocean and too deep to map with satellite imagery.

--- SENTENCE 2936 ---
The ~7.9 km<sup>2</sup> of mapped benthic habitat within the Monument is dominated by coral reef and hardbottom structures.

--- SENTENCE 2937 ---
which together comprise ~63% of the area within the Monument (Figure 5.62a).

--- SENTENCE 2938 ---
Coral reef structures comprise only ~10% of the mapped benthic habitat and include aggregate reef (~2%), aggregated patch reefs (~3%), and spur and groove (~5%).

--- SENTENCE 2939 ---
In comparison, these three structure types cover ~18% of the mapped benthic habitat around

### *Biogeographic Characteristics*

Rose Atoll MNM comprises a distinct biogeographic region (Bioregion 17, Chapter 4) that is a hotspot for fish biomass and has a unique coral community.

--- SENTENCE 2955 ---
Rose lies upstream in the South Equatorial Current relative to the rest of the Samoan Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 2956 ---
Analysis of larval connectivity in the region suggests that Rose Atoll may be isolated from larval sources and less resilient to disturbance (Chapter 3).

--- SENTENCE 2957 ---
Also of note, Rose Atoll is dominated by crustose coralline algae and possesses a unique algal community (Tribollet et al. 2010).

Wass 1981, Wass 1982, Green et al. 1997a, Wegmann and Holzwarth 2006, Schroeder et al. 2008, Tribollet et al. 2010, Vroom 2011

### **RESULTS: MPA NETWORK ANALYSES**

### **How much of American Samoa is protected in the MPA network?

--- SENTENCE 2958 ---
**

Approximately 427 km 2 of the nearshore area around American Samoa is shallower than 150 m and can be considered potential reef ecosystem as defined previously.

--- SENTENCE 2959 ---
As of January 2011, only ~8% (~32 km 2 ) of the potential reef ecosystem area around American Samoa is currently protected by the existing MPA network (Figure 5.64a).

--- SENTENCE 2960 ---
Considering the type of protection, only ~3% of the total potential reef ecosystem (~12 km 2 ) has complete no-take restrictions whereas ~5% (~20 km 2 ) has other regulations such as gear limits, devel opment restrictions, and bans on commercial fishing (Figure 5.64b).

### Which biogeographic regions and ecological hotspots are represented in the MPA network?

--- SENTENCE 2974 ---
Fourteen of the twenty ecologically distinct Bioregions identified in Chapter 4 include at least one MPA, leaving

only six with no representation in the present MPA network (Table 5.2).

--- SENTENCE 2975 ---
Bioregions not currently represented in the existing MPA network that have been identified as having unique reef fish and/or coral communities in Chapter 4 include only Swains Island (Bioregion 16) and Aunu'u (Bioregion 8).

--- SENTENCE 2976 ---
Overlaying the 36 ecological hotspots defined among the Bioregions with MPA boundaries revealed which hotspots are at least partly protected by the existing network.

--- SENTENCE 2977 ---
This simple accounting indicated that 25 out of 36 hotspots are at least partly protected by existing MPAs.

--- SENTENCE 2978 ---
Results were broadly consistent among all four variables (Table 5.2).

--- SENTENCE 2979 ---
Bioregions defined as hotspots for multiple variables may have greater ecological and conservation importance relative to regions that are hotspots for fewer variables.

--- SENTENCE 2980 ---
Seven of the Bioregions were defined as hotspots for 3 out of the 4 variables (none were hotspots for

--- SENTENCE 2993 ---
The simple hotspot and bioregional summaries presented above do not take into consideration two key fac tors that vary considerably among MPAs: size of the protected area and type of protection.

--- SENTENCE 2994 ---
The mere pres ence of an MPA within a Bioregion does not guarantee sufficient protection.

--- SENTENCE 2995 ---
For example, the SW coast of Tutuila (Bioregion 1, a hotspot for 3 of the 4 fish and coral variables) contains two of the existing MPAs and therefore it may appear that this Bioregion is being adequately protected with replication.

--- SENTENCE 2996 ---
However, the two MPAs in Bioregion 1 together comprise less than 0.4 km 2 of potential reef ecosystem, leaving the vast major ity of the Bioregion unprotected.

** Distribution of existing MPAs relative to the locations of significant ecological features, including Bioregions that are hotspots for three fish/coral variables (Chapter 4) and the mesophotic coral banks surrounding Tutuila (Appendix B).

### Size and regulatory comparisons among MPAs

MPAs around American Samoa have a very wide range of sizes and protect very different amounts of potential reef ecosystem, from <0.02 km<sup>2</sup> to ~9.1 km<sup>2</sup> (Figure 5.67, Table 5.3).

--- SENTENCE 2998 ---
It is important to consider both the proportions of habitats as well as their size when evaluating relative protection of coral reef and hardbottom features.

--- SENTENCE 2999 ---
For instance, many of the smallest MPAs possess a high proportion of coral reef and hardbottom structures, often greater than 80% of their area, and so encompass some key habitats very efficiently.

--- SENTENCE 3000 ---
However, they may not be large enough to encompass the home range of the fish species they are intended to protect.

--- SENTENCE 3001 ---
The largest MPAs generally encompass a wider variety of bottom types and therefore have lower proportions of coral reef and hardbottom, often less than 50% of their area.

--- SENTENCE 3002 ---
These low proportions of coral reef and hardbottom can be misleading in judging the relative contributions of an MPA and must be considered in the context of MPA size.

--- SENTENCE 3003 ---
For example, fifteen of the twenty-two MPAs were smaller than 1 km<sup>2</sup> individually.

--- SENTENCE 3004 ---
Collectively these fifteen MPAs encompass only ~25% of the protected coral reef and hardbottom (~4.4 km² of 16.9 km²) around American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3005 ---
In contrast, at Rose Atoll MNM, the largest MPA, only ~50% of the potential reef ecosystem is coral reef or hardbottom, but this encompasses 30% of the total protected coral reef and hardbottom around all of American Samoa (~5 km² out of 16.9 km²).

--- SENTENCE 3006 ---
This single MPA protects more coral reef and hardbottom than all 15 of the smallest MPAs combined.

--- SENTENCE 3007 ---
Larger MPAs are also more likely to be effective in protecting fish species with large home ranges.

!

--- SENTENCE 3008 ---
Proportion of potential reef ecosystem area by benthic structure type for each existing MPA.

--- SENTENCE 3009 ---
Pie sizes are scaled relative to potential reef ecosystem area.

--- SENTENCE 3010 ---
In addition to size differences, the MPAs around American Samoa vary in the type of protection they provide.

--- SENTENCE 3011 ---
Only two existing MPAs have at least some zone designated with the strongest level of protection, complete no-take.

--- SENTENCE 3012 ---
These include the entire Fagamalo No-Take MPA and the portion of the Rose Atoll MNM landward of the 50 fathom curve (including the NWR).

--- SENTENCE 3013 ---
These no-take areas comprise only ~3% of the area identified as coral reef habitat around American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3014 ---
In 2000, Governor Tauese Sunia set a goal to protect 20% of American Samoa's coral reefs in no-take areas by 2010 (Sunia 2000).

--- SENTENCE 3015 ---
Existing regulations can be modified or zones created within present MPAs in consultation with DMWR's No-Take MPA Program to partly meet this

goal.

--- SENTENCE 3038 ---
However, it should be noted that because only 10% of the total coral reef habitat in American Samoa is within existing MPAs, even if all were hypothetically re-zoned as no-take, the 20% goal would only be halfway achieved.

--- SENTENCE 3039 ---
New MPAs encompassing the same total area as all existing MPAs combined would need to be implemented.

--- SENTENCE 3040 ---
This hypothetical re-zoning example demonstrates that additional large, cross sectional MPAs with no-take restrictions such as recently implemented at Fagamalo by DMWR are necessary to accomplish this goal.

--- SENTENCE 3041 ---
In addition to establishing the no-take site at Fagamalo, DMWR's No-Take Program has identified additional priority sites (e.g. Aunu'u, Chapter 4), has conducted standardized biological surveys at those sites (Oram 2008), and plans continued engagement with other MPA programs and local communities to build strong commitments to achieving the 20% goal.

--- SENTENCE 3042 ---
As the MPA network expands under various authorities to meet this goal, MPA practitioners in American Samoa can use information in this assessment on larval connectivity (Chapter 3), fish and coral communities (Chapter 4), benthic features (Appendix B), and additional information to identify areas of high ecological value that could be added to the no-take components of the MPA network.

### **CONCLUSIONS**

Our goal in summarizing the biogeographic features within existing MPAs was to provide an accounting of the MPA landscape using a consistent set of broadly important ecosystem variables.

--- SENTENCE 3043 ---
However, it should be noted that spreading protection among Bioregions or in cluding representation of the particular ecologi cal hotspots defined in Chapter 4 is not an ex plicitly stated goal of the local MPA community in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3044 ---
It is up to this community to identify the specific goals to be achieved by the network and a process to achieve them.

--- SENTENCE 3045 ---
These goals may include biogeographic repre sentation, replication, quantitative targets (e.g. 20% no-take), ensuring connectivity among sites, and protection of specific ecological or cultural sites.

!

--- SENTENCE 3046 ---
** Original CFMP sign posted at Fagamalo.

--- SENTENCE 3047 ---
New signage was under development in 2011.

--- SENTENCE 3048 ---
Photo credit: Matt Kendall, NOAA Biogeography.

--- SENTENCE 3049 ---
While our analysis has focused on broad reef fish and coral variables, some existing MPAs

have little to do with these general ecological variables since they may be designed to protect cultural re sources or specific biota (e.g. Saua and Taisamasama cultural sites on Ta'u).

--- SENTENCE 3050 ---
There is a diversity of additional features of special importance that are worthy of protection that were not addressed using the general vari ables focused on in this study.

--- SENTENCE 3051 ---
For example, the mesophotic banks around Tutuila have some vibrant coral reef communities that may be less vulnerable to climate change and nearshore stressors than shallower reefs (Riegl and Piller 2003, Bare et al. 2010) but are poorly represented in the existing MPA network.

--- SENTENCE 3052 ---
Only the Fagamalo No-Take MPA encompasses such features around Tutuila presently.

--- SENTENCE 3053 ---
Another feature, Vailulu'u, the only volcanically active seamount of the 65 in the EEZs of American Samoa and Samoa and the origin of the Samoan archipelago, lies between Rose Atoll and the Manu'a Islands (Appendix A).

--- SENTENCE 3054 ---
Vailulu'u lies just outside the Rose Atoll MNM and a small boundary modification would encompass its unique hydrothermal vent communities and likely eventual emergence as a new island (Staudigel et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 3055 ---
Another example of a special and unique area is off the southwest coast of Ta'u and includes several coral heads of the spe cies *Porites lutea* (Fisk and Birkeland 2002, Brown et al. 2009) that are remarkable for their enormous size.

--- SENTENCE 3056 ---
These features presently lie outside the National Park boundary on Ta'u and are not currently protected by the existing MPA network.

--- SENTENCE 3057 ---
In addition to protecting such special or unique features at single sites, replication of non-unique regions or habitats at multiple sites that are similar is an important principle of MPA network design.

--- SENTENCE 3058 ---
This spreads the risk of degradation or loss of a particular ecosystem or resource over multiple MPAs and enhances the resiliency of the protected ecosystem or resource.

--- SENTENCE 3059 ---
Given the susceptibility of reef ecosystems to anthropo genic and natural disturbance (e.g. crown-of-thorns starfish, tsunamis, pollution), this should be an important consideration for MPA authorities in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3060 ---
For example, protecting Larsen Bay would almost perfectly replicate the very similar reef ecosystem in the adjacent and already protected Fagatele Bay NMS.

--- SENTENCE 3061 ---
Also, the discontinuous coral banks around Tutuila (e.g. Taema, Nafanua, and many others) offer abundant choices to replicate protection of bank features such as those in the Fagamalo No-Take MPA.

--- SENTENCE 3062 ---
Additional re gions lacking representation or replication in the existing network are identified in Table 5.2 and Figure 5.66.

--- SENTENCE 3063 ---
The connections among MPAs due to factors such as swimming within a home range for adult fish, onto genic habitat shifts, and dispersal of fish and coral larvae are also important to consider in network design.

--- SENTENCE 3064 ---
Telemetry or tagging studies are needed to quantify the scales and frequencies of fish movements among and across MPA boundaries.

--- SENTENCE 3065 ---
Hydrodynamic models are needed to quantify connections among MPAs due to larval dispersal.

--- SENTENCE 3066 ---
Chapter 3 of this assessment used a broad-scale hydrodynamic model to evaluate connec tions among islands of the entire archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 3067 ---
It was found that Samoa's much larger islands and coral reef area are the largest source of larvae in the archipelago, some of which are circulated to American Samoa via the South Equatorial Counter Current (Chapter 3).

--- SENTENCE 3068 ---
Most of American Samoa (except Swains Island) in turn, lies upstream of Samoa in the South Equatorial Current and many larvae spawned in MPAs there may seed the reefs of Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3069 ---
Distances between islands and MPAs and their positions in these currents partly dictate which species of larvae may be spawned in one MPA but then be transported to sustain the resident population in another.

--- SENTENCE 3070 ---
For example, the MPA at Rose Atoll MNM not only encompasses a large proportion of the protected reef habitats in American Samoa, but connectivity models indicate that some of its larval production gets exported downstream to the other islands in the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 3071 ---
All existing and proposed MPAs should be evaluated in a similar context.

--- SENTENCE 3072 ---
For maximum benefit, the broad scale hydrodynamic models in this assessment must be coupled with finer-scale models to understand smaller current patterns and eddies around particular islands.

--- SENTENCE 3073 ---
DMWR and ASEPA are presently developing such a model around Tutuila to better understand localized larval transport and identify potential MPA sites that may provide resilient sources of larvae to the broader ecosystem and MPA network.

--- SENTENCE 3074 ---
A comprehensive and cooperative MPA strategy with protection goals that involves the many MPA manage ment programs in American Samoa has been developed over the past 5 years (Oram 2006, 2008, Damitz 2007).

--- SENTENCE 3075 ---
The strategy consists of 5 action plans covering governance and administration, MPA designation, education and outreach, research and monitoring, and enforcement, with each containing time-bound goals and objectives.

--- SENTENCE 3076 ---
One of the key overarching goals is the need for effective dialogue and collaboration among MPA programs to most effectively align resource protection needs with appropriate management authorities.

--- SENTENCE 3077 ---
Effective communication and collaboration among MPA programs is vital to not only minimize stakeholder confusion at the village level but also to prevent competitive obstruction among many well intending agencies working in a relatively small region.

--- SENTENCE 3078 ---
To date, the MPA network strategy has facilitated the establishment of an MPA Coordinator within the Territorial government's Coral Reef Advisory Group and the formation of an MPA Network Working Group.

--- SENTENCE 3079 ---
Key next steps in the evolution of the MPA network strategy are to, 1) define over all quantitative resource protection goals, some of which have been stated by individual programs already such as establishment of 20% of coral reefs as no-take refugia (Oram 2008), 2) quantify what resources are protected within the existing MPA network (i.e. this analysis and others like it, e.g. Fisk and Birkeland 2002, NOAA NOS 2009), 3) identify ways to accomplish resource protection goals through additional MPAs or mod ification of the size, regulations, or spatial arrangement of existing MPAs, and 4) identify which management authorities, or more likely which mix of programs, can contribute the appropriate combination of financial, material, and stakeholder support roles necessary to successfully manage each site and the overall network.

--- SENTENCE 3080 ---
The MPA network in American Samoa is an ever-changing landscape.

--- SENTENCE 3081 ---
As of January 2011, a number of po tential MPAs were at various stages in the "proposal" process.

--- SENTENCE 3082 ---
These MPAs would add potential reef ecosys tem and additional coral reef and hardbottom areas to the MPA network and may protect Bioregions, ecologi cal hotspots, or special features noted in this report that are not currently protected by the existing network.

--- SENTENCE 3083 ---
It is also important to note that MPAs in American Samoa vary considerably in terms of their permanence.

--- SENTENCE 3084 ---
Some provide fixed protection in perpetuity barring new legislation and others provide more changeable protection for shorter durations at which point they must be renewed.

--- SENTENCE 3085 ---
Many CFMP reserves, for instance, are established for an initial period of 2-3 years, after which modifications to protection level, boundaries and regulations can be made by village leaders in response to changing needs and resource conditions.

--- SENTENCE 3086 ---
As the MPA landscape around American Samoa evolves, the components of this Biogeographic Assessment can be used to evaluate the ecological contributions of additions to the network on the basis of protected habitats (Appendix B), reef fish and coral communities (Chapter 4 and Appendix C), and larval connectivity (Chapter 3).

--- SENTENCE 3087 ---
Once comprehensive benthic maps and MPA boundary files become available for the Samoan Islands of Upolu and Savai'i, a full accounting of the MPA Network for both American Samoa and Samoa will be pos sible.

--- SENTENCE 3088 ---
This is a critical next step in regional MPA planning given the close proximity and high potential for interdependency of MPAs across both these jurisdictions.

--- SENTENCE 3089 ---
A comprehensive, archipelago-wide MPA strategy is recommended that maximizes the benefits of this potential connectivity and promotes resiliency of not only the wider MPA network, but also coral reef ecosystems more generally throughout the archipelago.

# **Appendix A: Seamounts within the exclusive economic zones of Samoa and American Samoa**

Laurie B. Bauer1 and Matthew S. Kendall2

### **IntroduCtIon**

Seamounts are underwater mountains of volcanic origin.

--- SENTENCE 3333 ---
They are often formed near mid-ocean ridges or subduction zones at the edges of tectonic plates but also occur over upwelling plumes ("hotspots") within plate boundaries (Wessel 2001).

--- SENTENCE 3334 ---
Like all geologic formations, seamounts change in shape and height over millions of years as a result of the gradual processes of volcanic growth upward out of the seafloor, growth of coral reefs if emergent or shallow enough, and eventually the processes of erosion and subsidence or sinking of the reshaped structure back into the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 3335 ---
The Samoan Archipelago is part of a hotspot chain

!

--- SENTENCE 3336 ---
** Seamounts of the Samoan Exclusive Economic Zones.

--- SENTENCE 3337 ---
<sup>1</sup>NOAA/NOS/NCCOS/CCMA/Biogeography Branch and Consolidated Safety Services, Inc., Fairfax, VA, under NOAA Contract No. DG133C07NC0616

<sup>2</sup> NOAA/NOS/NCCOS/CCMA Biogeography Branch

that extends from the volcanically active Vailulu'u seamount in the east to west of the island of Savai'i (Hart et al. 2006) and includes examples of many stages in the seamount life cycle.

--- SENTENCE 3338 ---
The region also includes many seamounts not associated with the Samoan hotspot (Figure A.1).

--- SENTENCE 3339 ---
Seamounts are not only interesting features geologically as described above, but also biologically in that they represent oases of biodiversity relative to the comparatively barren ocean and seafloor surrounding them.

--- SENTENCE 3340 ---
Seamounts offer an array of habitat opportunities, current fields, and depth zones for plankton, fish and corals to occupy, they play a role as "stepping stone" features connecting populations of reef fish and corals between islands, are known gathering sites for many pelagic fish species, and consequently are popular destinations for fishing and scientific study (Rogers 1994).

--- SENTENCE 3341 ---
The scientific definition of a seamount has evolved over time (Staudigel et al. 2010) and has variously been based on some minimum height above the seafloor, gravity anomalies of even fully subsided or buried seamounts, and has included emergent islands by some definitions.

--- SENTENCE 3342 ---
In this assessment seamounts are defined as totally submerged but extending a minimum of 150 m above the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 3343 ---
The objective of this appendix is to provide a characterization of seamounts within the Exclusive Economic Zones (EEZs) of Samoa and American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3344 ---
Of particular importance are those shallow enough to be colonized by reef fish and corals.

### **MethodS**

Seamounts are typically mapped using sonar and satellite altimetry.

--- SENTENCE 3345 ---
While sonar based mapping is the most direct method and provides detailed resolution, it is expensive and generally limited in spatial coverage.

--- SENTENCE 3346 ---
Satellite altimetry in contrast, which can be used to detect seamounts indirectly due to variations in the Earth's gravity field (Wessel 2001, Wessel et al. 2010), is available at a global scale but is comparatively coarse resolution.

--- SENTENCE 3347 ---
Two datasets were used to characterize seamounts in the Samoan Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 3348 ---
The Global Seamount Census (Wessel 2001, Wessel et al. 2010) is a global database of ~12,000 seamount features from satellite-derived bathymetry (Smith and Sandwell 1997).

--- SENTENCE 3349 ---
The Seamount Biogeosciences Network provides a characterization for ~1,800 seamounts worldwide, including available multibeam data, relevant literature, and morphological characteristics (Koppers et al. 2010a).

--- SENTENCE 3350 ---
Seamounts from both sources were plotted and examined in concert with available bathymetry datasets.

--- SENTENCE 3351 ---
Three bathymetry data sources were used

!

--- SENTENCE 3352 ---
** Frequency distribution of seamounts within the Samoan and American Samoan EEZ based on a) depth of seamount top and b) height.

to obtain complete coverage of the study region.

--- SENTENCE 3353 ---
High resolution (5-40 m) bathymetry based on multibeam sonar was obtained from the Pacific Islands Benthic Habitat Mapping Center (http://www.soest.hawaii.edu/pibhmc.htm) for three seamounts: Vailulu'u, Muli (locally known as Northeast Bank) and Tulaga (Two-Percent Bank).

--- SENTENCE 3354 ---
Moderate resolution (180 m) bathymetry from merged multibeam and satellite data were downloaded from Koppers et al. (2010a).

--- SENTENCE 3355 ---
Last, one-minute bathymetry estimated from satellite altimetry, ship depth soundings, and other sources (http://topex.ucsd.edu, Smith and Sandwell 1997), was used where the two finer resolution datasets lacked coverage.

--- SENTENCE 3356 ---
In many cases, seamount locations disagreed slightly between the two data sources and were therefore moved slightly in this characterization to more precisely identify the approximate peak of each seamount based on bathymetry.

--- SENTENCE 3357 ---
A few features from the satellite derived dataset (Wessel et al. 2010) lacked significant bathymetric relief and were removed.

--- SENTENCE 3358 ---
These features may have been gravity anomalies representing "buried" seamounts (Wessel 2001, Wessel et al. 2010).

--- SENTENCE 3359 ---
Additional features not included in either seamount dataset were identified within the EEZs of Samoa and American Samoa based on the bathymetry.

--- SENTENCE 3360 ---
Summary information for each seamount feature was compiled including peak coordinates, depth of peak, and total height above the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 3361 ---
The depth of the peak and base of the seamount was estimated in ArcGIS and the height was calculated as the difference between these estimates.

--- SENTENCE 3362 ---
Seamounts are summarized in tabular form, as histograms based on peak height and depth, and in map format for both the region and as individual maps for features with potential reef communities.

### **reSultS

--- SENTENCE 3363 ---
And dISCuSSIon**

A total of 65 seamount features were identified; 48 within the EEZ of American Samoa, 16 within the EEZ of Samoa, and one, Tisa Seamount situated on the EEZ boundary (Figure A.1; Table A.1).

--- SENTENCE 3364 ---
Approximately 20 of the seamounts in the study area are derived from the Samoan hotspot and lie along the axis of the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 3365 ---
This group includes many of the largest and shallowest seamounts in the region.

--- SENTENCE 3366 ---
In addition, there are two groups of smaller seamounts in the southeastern and northern regions of the American Samoa EEZ.

!

Peak depth ranged from 23 m to deeper than 5,000 m.

--- SENTENCE 3494 ---
The frequency of peak height by depth exhibited a bi-modal pattern, with the majority of seamount peaks located in water over >2000 m in depth (Figure A.2a).

--- SENTENCE 3495 ---
The majority of seamounts (60%) were less than 1,500 m in height (Figure A.2b).

--- SENTENCE 3496 ---
Only Vailulu'u seamount (Figure A.3) is characterized as hydrothermally active, whereas the remaining seamounts are extinct volcanoes (Koppers et al. 2010b).

--- SENTENCE 3497 ---
The biological community varies among different locations on Vailulu'u and includes polychaetes, crinoids, octocorals, sponges, and a population of cutthroat eels (Staudigel et al. 2006).

--- SENTENCE 3498 ---
In general, there is a lack of information on biological communities for other seamounts within the EEZ of Samoa and American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 3499 ---
Seamounts Online (Stocks 2009) is a global database of user-contributed data on species distributions on seamounts.

--- SENTENCE 3500 ---
However, there was no data available for seamounts on the Samoan Archipelago at this time.

--- SENTENCE 3501 ---
The estimated depths of the top of the seamount features were used to determine whether shallow or mesophotic reefs were potentially present.

--- SENTENCE 3502 ---
Generally, mesophotic reefs range from ~30 to 150 m depth, although deeper records of zoxanthellate corals and coralline algae have been documented (Hinderstein et al. 2010).

--- SENTENCE 3503 ---
The estimated peaks of the seamount features were only shallower than 150 m depth for 5 out of 65 features, suggesting that mesophotic reefs are potentially present.

--- SENTENCE 3504 ---
Three of these seamounts are located within the American Samoa EEZ (Figures A.4-A.6) while the latter two features are located on the western edge of the Samoa EEZ (Figures A.7-A.8).

--- SENTENCE 3505 ---
Paputua Guyot, locally known as South Bank, has been identified as a drowned atoll in recent bathymetric surveys although development of mesophotic reef communities is lacking (R. Brainard, personal communication, NOAA Coral Reef Ecosystem Division, Honolulu, HI).

--- SENTENCE 3506 ---
All the remaining seamount features are estimated to be greater than 300 m deep, although actual depths should be interpreted with caution due to the scale and estimation methods of the bathymetry data.

--- SENTENCE 3507 ---
For example, there was a large difference in the depth of Tulaga Seamount, locally known as East Bank, when measured by multibeam sonar (78 m, PIBHMC) versus satellite altimetry (> 700 m).

### **ConCluSIonS**

A wide range of seamount morphologies exist within the Samoan EEZs.

--- SENTENCE 3508 ---
Approximately one third of the seamounts in the study area are derived from the Samoan hotspot which is presently located at Vailulu'u with the rest scattered in two main groups in the American Samoa EEZ.

--- SENTENCE 3509 ---
The five seamount features with potential mesophotic reef communities were evaluated further and used as inputs for understanding reef connectivity in Chapter 3 of this assessment.

### **ACknowledgeMentS**

The seamount databases available online and cited in this document are an excellent resource.

--- SENTENCE 3510 ---
We gratefully acknowledge Dr. Anthony Koppers for allowing us permission to use the original seamount maps available at EarthRef.org in our report.

#

--- SENTENCE 3534 ---
**Appendix B: Shoreline to Shelf edge Benthic Maps of tutuila, American Samoa**

Matthew S. Kendall1

--- SENTENCE 3535 ---
### **IntroduCtIon**

Accurate maps of coral reef ecosystems are a critical component of reef science and management.

--- SENTENCE 3536 ---
Me sophotic reefs around Tutuila, American Samoa makeup the majority of the area of coral reef ecosystems around the island but have not been comprehensively mapped and classified like their shallow water coun terparts (see NOAA NCCOS 2005).

--- SENTENCE 3537 ---
To meet this need we created benthic maps of the mesophotic reef eco systems and edge matched them to the existing shallow water maps to produce a comprehensive shoreline to shelf edge map of benthic features.

--- SENTENCE 3538 ---
### **MethodS**

Benthic features were visually interpreted from sonar imagery collected by the Pacific Islands Benthic Habi tat Mapping Center (PIBHMC) (downloaded from http://www.soest.hawaii.edu/pibhmc/).

--- SENTENCE 3539 ---
Primary datasets used during map production were bathymetry and backscatter.

--- SENTENCE 3540 ---
Additional image datasets derived from these primary sources were also used during interpretation and included slope, rugosity, contours, and hillshade.

--- SENTENCE 3541 ---
Areas of consistent tone and texture in the sonar imagery were identified visually by toggling among the vari ous sonar datalayers.

--- SENTENCE 3542 ---
Boundaries were drawn around these contiguous sonar signatures using the Habitat Digitizer Extension to ArcGIS 9.0 (http://ccma.nos.noaa.gov/products/biogeography/digitizer/welcome.html).

--- SENTENCE 3543 ---
All features were delineated at a scale of 1:10,000.

--- SENTENCE 3544 ---
The minimum mapping unit (MMU- size of the smallest feature to be delineated) was restricted to 4,000 m2 to be consistent with benthic maps recently produced for shallow water areas (NOAA NCCOS 2005).

--- SENTENCE 3545 ---
Benthic features with consistent sonar signatures were attributed based on a classification scheme modi fied from the recently completed "Benthic Habitats of American Samoa, Guam, and CNMI" (NOAA NCCOS 2005).

--- SENTENCE 3546 ---
The scheme was originally designed for use with color satellite imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3547 ---
The spatial properties of the satellite and sonar imagery (i.e. 5 m bathymetry and 1 m back scatter grid resolution for sonar imagery versus 4 m color and 1 m black and white grid resolution for IKONOS satellite imagery) and scales of map ping were similar (i.e. on-screen digitizing scale was 1:10K for sonar imagery and 1:6K for satellite imagery, MMU was 4,000 m<sup>2</sup> for both data sources).

--- SENTENCE 3548 ---
Sonar signatures were primarily ground truthed using video transect data from a towed camera system which was supplemented with drop camera video on specific features.

--- SENTENCE 3549 ---
Video transect data was collected between 2002 and 2006 and obtained from PIBHMC (Bare et al. 2010).

--- SENTENCE 3550 ---
Supplemental drop camera data was collected in May 2010 and consisted of 119 sites on features in between the video transects.

--- SENTENCE 3551 ---
Each site was characterized by ~2 minutes of video for a total of ~4 hours of seafloor imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3552 ---
A key goal was to create comprehensive maps of the coral reef ecosystem of Tutuila from the shoreline to the shelf edge.

--- SENTENCE 3553 ---
The extent of the sonar data was approximately from the insular shelf edge to the base of the fringing reefs around Tutuila, however, in places it did not cover all the way to the shelf edge, include the base of fringing reefs (lower fore reef), or necessarily include 100% coverage throughout the shelf.

--- SENTENCE 3554 ---
Gaps between sonar swaths on the shelf were simply ignored during digitizing since they were typically narrow and occurred in regions of relatively homogenous seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 3555 ---
To fill in gaps in coverage at the shelf edge, the 100 fathom isobath from NOAA Navigational Chart #83484 was overlayed in the GIS and used to assist in digi tizing placement of the shelf edge polygon.

--- SENTENCE 3556 ---
The gap in coverage between the sonar data and the shoreline was filled using maps from the 2005 Benthic Habitats of American Samoa, Guam and CNMI data CD (NOAA NCCOS 2005).

--- SENTENCE 3557 ---
The satellite base maps (NOAA NCCOS 2005) were edge matched to sonar based maps principally along the seaward edge of the fore reef, a feature often visible in both sonar and satellite imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3558 ---
The shallow water map was generally clipped out in regions of overlap due to the higher interpretability of the sonar imagery of this zone relative to the satellite imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3559 ---
<sup>1</sup> NOAA/NOS/NCCOS/CCMA Biogeography Branch

### **Classification Scheme**

The classification scheme defined benthic habitats based on four attributes: 1) shelf zone, 2) general geomorphological structure, 3) detailed structure, and 4) percent hardbottom.

--- SENTENCE 3560 ---
Every feature in the benthic habitat map was assigned a designation from each level of the scheme.

--- SENTENCE 3561 ---
We customized the classification scheme to be compatible with, 1) the available sonar data for American Samoa, and 2) the existing benthic maps of shallow reefs for American Samoa (NOAA NCCOS 2005).

--- SENTENCE 3562 ---
The primary differences between this scheme and the one used to map shallow reefs of Tutuila were that biological cover was not mapped whereas percent hardbottom was.

--- SENTENCE 3563 ---
These changes were necessary to the scheme due to the differences in sonar and satellite imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3564 ---
### Zones

Thirteen mutually exclusive zones were identified from land to deep ocean corresponding to typical insular shelf and coral reef geomorphology.

--- SENTENCE 3565 ---
These zones included: Land, Shoreline Intertidal, Reef Flat, Lagoon, Back Reef, Reef Crest, Fore Reef, Bank/Shelf, Bank/Shelf Basin, Bank/Shelf Escarpment, Channel, Dredged, and Unknown.

--- SENTENCE 3566 ---
Figure B.1 illustrates zone types across a typical cross-section of the island shelf.

--- SENTENCE 3567 ---
Zone refers only to each benthic community's location and does not address substrate or structure types that are found within.

--- SENTENCE 3568 ---
For example, the Lagoon zone may include patch reefs, sand, and reef rubble; however, these are considered structural elements that may or may not occur within the lagoon zone and therefore, are not used to define it.

--- SENTENCE 3569 ---
Note that some zone categories exist only in the nearshore map (NOAA NCCOS 2005; e.g. shoreline/intertidal and reef crest) and others only exist in the sonar based portion of the map (e.g. bank/shelf escarpment and bank/shelf basin).

--- SENTENCE 3570 ---
See NOAA NCCOS (2005) and Bare et al. (2010) for additional cross sectional figures and example photographs of each zone type.

!

--- SENTENCE 3571 ---
** Cross section of Zones.

--- SENTENCE 3572 ---
### *Land*

Terrestrial features at or near the spring high tide line.

--- SENTENCE 3573 ---
The shoreline is based on the official digital shoreline available at the time nearshore mapping was conducted (NOAA NCCOS 2005).

--- SENTENCE 3574 ---
As a result many of the land polygons may be smaller than the MMU used to delineate marine features.

### *Shoreline Intertidal*

Area between the spring high tide line (or landward edge of emergent vegetation when present) and lowest spring tide level.

--- SENTENCE 3575 ---
Emergent segments of reefs are excluded from this zone and instead are defined below as Reef Crest.

--- SENTENCE 3576 ---
Typically, this zone is narrow due to the small tidal range in Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 3577 ---
While present island-wide, the feature is often too narrow to be mapped on steep shorelines due to the scale of the imagery and the MMU.

--- SENTENCE 3578 ---
### *Lagoon*

Shallow area (relative to the deeper water of the bank/shelf) between the Shoreline Intertidal zone and the Back Reef of a reef.

--- SENTENCE 3579 ---
This zone is typically protected from the high-energy waves commonly experienced on the Bank/Shelf and Reef Crest zones.

### *Reef Flat*

Shallow, semi-exposed area of little relief between the Shoreline Intertidal zone and the Reef Crest of a fring ing reef.

--- SENTENCE 3580 ---
This broad, flat area often exists just landward of a Reef Crest and may extend to the shoreline or drop into a Lagoon.

--- SENTENCE 3581 ---
This zone is often somewhat protected from the high-energy waves commonly experi enced on the Bank/Shelf and Reef Crest zones. 

### *Back Reef*

Area just landward of a Reef Crest that slopes downward towards the seaward edge of a Lagoon floor or Bank/Shelf.

--- SENTENCE 3582 ---
This zone is present only when a Reef Crest exists.

### *Reef Crest*

The uppermost, and often flattened, emergent (especially during low tides) or nearly emergent segment of a reef.

--- SENTENCE 3583 ---
This high wave energy zone lies between the Fore Reef and Back Reef or Reef Flat zones.

--- SENTENCE 3584 ---
Breaking waves are often visible in aerial or satellite imagery at the seaward edge of this zone.

--- SENTENCE 3585 ---
### *Fore Reef*

Area along the seaward edge of the Reef Crest that slopes into deeper water to the landward edge of the Bank/Shelf platform.

--- SENTENCE 3586 ---
This feature is often referred to locally as the reef slope.

--- SENTENCE 3587 ---
Features not associated with an emergent Reef Crest but still having a seaward-facing slope that is significantly greater than the slope of the Bank/Shelf are also designated as Fore Reef.

### *Bank/Shelf*

Deeper water area (relative to the shallow water in a lagoon or reef flat) extending offshore from the seaward edge of the Fore Reef or shoreline to the beginning of the escarpment where the insular shelf drops off into deep, oceanic water.

--- SENTENCE 3588 ---
If no Reef Crest is present, the Bank/Shelf is the flattened platform between the Fore Reef and deep open ocean waters or between the Shoreline Intertidal zone and open ocean.

### *Bank/Shelf Basin*

Broad depressions of deeper water occurring in the Bank/Shelf.

--- SENTENCE 3589 ---
These features are surrounded by well de fined inflections in bathymetry up to the relatively less deep waters of the Bank/Shelf. 

### *Bank/Shelf Escarpment*

This zone begins on the oceanic edge of the Bank/Shelf, where depth increases rapidly into deep, oceanic water and exceeds the depth limit of sonar imagery around Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 3590 ---
This zone is intended to capture the in flection point of the shelf to deep waters of the open ocean. 

### *Channel*

Naturally occurring channels that often cut across several other zones.

### *Dredged*

Area in which natural geomorphology is disrupted or altered by excavation or dredging.

### *Pinnacle*

High relief features occurring in or adjacent to Bank/Shelf Basin that are capped by coral reef or hard bottom.

--- SENTENCE 3591 ---
### *Unknown*

Zone indistinguishable due to gaps between swaths in sonar imagery or due to turbidity, cloud cover, water depth, or other interference in satellite imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3592 ---
### Geomorphological Structures

Fifteen distinct and non-overlapping geomorphologic structure could be mapped by visual interpretation of sonar imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3593 ---
Structure refers only to predominate physical composition of the feature and does not address location (see Zone for shore to shelf edge location).

--- SENTENCE 3594 ---
The structure types are defined in a collapsible hierarchy ranging from four major classes (Coral Reef and Hardbottom, Unconsolidated Substrate, Other Delineations, and Unknown), to fifteen detailed classes listed and defined below.

--- SENTENCE 3595 ---
See NOAA NCCOS (2005) for example photographs and satellite images of each classification.

### *Coral Reef and Hardbottom*

Solid substrates including bedrock, boulders, and reef building organisms.

--- SENTENCE 3596 ---
A thin veneer of sediment may be present.

--- SENTENCE 3597 ---
Detailed classes within this category include Aggregate Reef, Individual Patch Reef, Aggregated Patch Reefs, Spur and Groove, Pavement, Pavement with Sand Channels, Pavement with Patch Reefs, Reef Rubble, and Rock/Boulder.

--- SENTENCE 3598 ---
### Aggregate Reef

Continuous, high-relief coral formation of variable shapes lacking sand channels of Spur and Groove.

--- SENTENCE 3599 ---
Includes linear coral formations that are oriented parallel to shore or the shelf edge.

--- SENTENCE 3600 ---
This class is used for such commonly referred to terms as linear reef, fore reef or fringing reef.

### Individual Patch Reef

Patch reefs are coral formations that are isolated from other coral reef formations by bare sand, seagrass, or other habitats and that have no organized structural axis relative to the contours of the shore or shelf edge.

--- SENTENCE 3601 ---
They are characterized by an often circular or oblong shape with a vertical relief of one meter or more in relation to the surrounding seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 3602 ---
Individual Patch Reefs are larger than or equal to the MMU.

--- SENTENCE 3603 ---
Patch Reefs

These features have the same defining characteristics as an Individual Patch Reef.

--- SENTENCE 3604 ---
However, this class refers to clustered patch reefs that individually are too small (less than the MMU) or are too close together to map separately.

--- SENTENCE 3605 ---
Where aggregated patch reefs share sand halos, the halo is included in the polygon.

### Spur and Groove

This structure has alternating sand and coral formations that are oriented perpendicular to the shore or reef crest.

--- SENTENCE 3606 ---
The coral formations (spurs) of this feature typically have a high vertical relief relative to pavement with sand channels and are separated from each other by 1-5 meters of sand or hardbottom (grooves), although the height and width of these elements may vary considerably.

--- SENTENCE 3607 ---
This habitat type typically occurs in the Fore Reef or Bank/Shelf Escarpment zone.

--- SENTENCE 3608 ---
### Pavement

Flat, low-relief, solid rock in broad areas often with partial coverage of sand, algae, hard coral, gorgonians, zooanthids or other sessile invertebrates.

--- SENTENCE 3609 ---
### Pavement with Sand Channels

Areas of pavement with alternating sand/surge channel formations that are oriented perpendicular to the shore or Bank/Shelf Escarpment.

--- SENTENCE 3610 ---
The sand/surge channels of this feature have low vertical relief relative to Spur and Groove formations.

--- SENTENCE 3611 ---
This habitat type occurs in areas exposed to moderate wave surge such as the Bank/Shelf zone.

--- SENTENCE 3612 ---
### Pavement with Patch Reefs

Habitats of pavement with occasional patch reef formations that make up less than 10% of the area of the polygon.

--- SENTENCE 3613 ---
This habitat type occurs nested in pavement areas on the Bank/Shelf zone.

### Reef Rubble

Dead, unstable coral rubble often colonized with turf, filamentous, calcareous, or encrusting macroalgae.

--- SENTENCE 3614 ---
This habitat often occurs landward of well developed reef formations in the Reef Crest, Back Reef or Reef Flat zones due to storm waves piling up dead coral.

--- SENTENCE 3615 ---
Reef Rubble can also occur in offshore areas due to diseased or physically impacted reef communities.

### Rock/Boulder

Large, irregularly shaped carbonate blocks or volcanic rock often extending offshore from the island bedrock or headlands.

--- SENTENCE 3616 ---
Can also occur as aggregations of loose rock fragments that have been detached and transported from their native beds.

--- SENTENCE 3617 ---
Individual boulders often range in diameter from 0.25 – 3 m.

### *Unconsolidated Substrate*

Areas of the seafloor consisting of small, unattached or uncemented particles with less than 10% cover of large stable substrate.

--- SENTENCE 3618 ---
Detailed structure classes of softbottom include Sand, Mud, Sand with Scattered Coral and Rock, and Algal Plain.

### Sand

Coarse sediment typically found in areas exposed to currents or wave energy.

--- SENTENCE 3619 ---
### Mud

Fine sediment often associated with stream discharge and build-up of organic material in areas sheltered from high-energy waves and currents.

### Sand with Scattered Coral and Rock

--- SENTENCE 3620 ---
Primarily sand bottom with scattered rocks or small, isolated coral heads that are too small to be delineated individually (i.e. smaller than individual patch reefs).

--- SENTENCE 3621 ---
If the density of small coral heads is greater than 10% of the entire polygon, this structure type is described as Aggregated Patch Reefs.

### Algal Pain

Low relief (<~0.25 m) substrate composed of a mixture of sand, live halimeda, halimeda sand, fleshy macroalgae, and rhodoliths.

--- SENTENCE 3622 ---
Relative abundance of these compositional elements is highly variable over scales of a few meters.

### *Other Delineations*

Any other type of structure not classified as Coral Reef and Hardbottom or Unconsolidated Sediment.

--- SENTENCE 3623 ---
Usually related to the terrestrial environment and/or anthropogenic activity.

--- SENTENCE 3624 ---
Detailed structure classes include Land, Artificial, and Emergent Vegetation.

### Land

Terrestrial features at or near the spring high tide line.

### Artificial

Man-made habitats such as submerged wrecks, large piers, submerged portions of rip-rap jetties, and the shoreline of islands created from dredge spoil.

### Emergent Vegetation

This category includes all species of mangroves regardless of canopy density.

--- SENTENCE 3625 ---
This class was not used the sonar derived map for obvious reasons but was retained in the classification scheme due to its occurrence in nearshore maps.

### *Unknown*

Major structure indistinguishable due to data gaps from turbidity, cloud cover, water depth, ship orientation, line spacing, or other interference with an interpretable signature of the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 3626 ---
Unknown

Detailed structure indistinguishable as above.

--- SENTENCE 3627 ---
### *Percent Hardbottom*

Seven classes were used to denote the approximate proportion of each polygon occupied by hard bottom substrate.

--- SENTENCE 3628 ---
A polygon encompassing several patch reefs that were too small to be delineated individually is actually comprised of some area of patch reefs and some background structure such as sand.

--- SENTENCE 3629 ---
This category includes both "living" hard bottom such as patch reefs as well as "abiotic" features such as pavement and rock/boulder.

--- SENTENCE 3630 ---
This attribute can be used to estimate the combined amount of coral reef and hard bottom around the island based on the area of each polygon.

--- SENTENCE 3631 ---
<10%

Used for all sand, mud, and sand with scattered coral and rock polygons.

--- SENTENCE 3632 ---
10-30%

Used for some aggregated patch reef polygons and other discontinuous features.

--- SENTENCE 3633 ---
30-50%

Used for some aggregated patch reef polygons and other discontinuous features.

--- SENTENCE 3634 ---
50-70%

Used for some aggregated patch reef polygons and other discontinuous features.

--- SENTENCE 3635 ---
70-90%

Used for some aggregated patch reef polygons, pavement, and other discontinuous features.

--- SENTENCE 3636 ---
90-100%

Used for most individual patch reef, pavement, aggregate reef polygons, and other continuous features.

--- SENTENCE 3637 ---
10-90%

This broad category was used for algal plain polygons due to the high variability in Rhodolith coverage of this bottom type which was not interpretable in the sonar imagery.

--- SENTENCE 3638 ---
Rhodoliths are hard algal nodules with ~5-10 cm diameter that are not cemented to the seafloor.

--- SENTENCE 3639 ---
They can form highly variable coverage from sparse (a few per square meter) to a nearly continuous cover over sand substrates.

--- SENTENCE 3640 ---
### **reSultS**

### **ACknowledgeMentS**

Sonar data and towed camera imagery are essential tools for mapping coral reef ecosystems at this depth and are the basis of this mapping project in deeper waters.

--- SENTENCE 3641 ---
These data were collected and made available for this project by staff at the Pacific Islands Benthic Habitat Mapping Center and NOAA's NMFS/PIFSC/Coral Reef Ecosystem Division.

--- SENTENCE 3642 ---
We are particularly grateful to John Rooney, Alica Bare, and Kerry Grimshaw for obtaining and navigating those datasets.

--- SENTENCE 3643 ---
Laurie Bauer, Kim Roberson, Kevin Grant, Chris Caldow, and Ken Buja were intrumental in collecting additional ground validation data.

--- SENTENCE 3644 ---
Ken Buja provided help with the habitat digitizer and with sewing the shallow and deep water maps together.
